</history><pub-history><event event-type="pmc-release"><date><day>07</day><month>07</month><year>2023</year></date></event><event event-type="pmc-live"><date><day>08</day><month>07</month><year>2023</year></date></event><event event-type="pmc-last-change"><date iso-8601-date="2023-07-27 16:25:10.030"><day>27</day><month>07</month><year>2023</year></date></event></pub-history><permissions><license><ali:license_ref xmlns:ali="http://www.niso.org/schemas/ali/1.0/">https://ehp.niehs.nih.gov/about-ehp/license</ali:license_ref><license-p><italic toggle="yes">EHP</italic> is an open-access journal published with support from the National Institute of Environmental Health Sciences, National Institutes of Health. All content is public domain unless otherwise noted.</license-p></license></permissions><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="pmc-pdf" xlink:href="ehp11885.pdf"><?pdf-name ehp11885.pdf?><?pdf-size 1763645?><?pdf-md5 4e72f28d348bb914ab6023dca185544d?><?pdf-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?pdf-cloudpmc-urn urn:app:0b50/10328216/4e72f28d348b/ehp11885.pdf?></self-uri><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="pdf" xlink:href="ehp11885.pdf"/><abstract><sec><title>Background:</title><p>Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) are ubiquitous in the environment and have been shown to cause various adverse health impacts. In animals, sex- and species-specific differences in PFAS elimination half-lives have been linked to the activity of kidney transporters. However, PFAS molecular interactions with kidney transporters are still not fully understood. Moreover, the impact of kidney disease on PFAS elimination remains unclear.</p></sec><sec><title>Objectives:</title><p>This state-of-the-science review integrated current knowledge to assess how changes in kidney function and transporter expression from health to disease could affect PFAS toxicokinetics and identified priority research gaps that should be addressed to advance knowledge.</p></sec><sec><title>Methods:</title><p>We searched for studies that measured PFAS uptake by kidney transporters, quantified transporter-level changes associated with kidney disease status, and developed PFAS pharmacokinetic models. We then used two databases to identify untested kidney transporters that have the potential for PFAS transport based on their endogenous substrates. Finally, we used an existing pharmacokinetic model for perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) in male rats to explore the influence of transporter expression levels, glomerular filtration rate (GFR), and serum albumin on serum half-lives.</p></sec><sec><title>Results:</title><p>The literature search identified nine human and eight rat kidney transporters that were previously investigated for their ability to transport PFAS, as well as seven human and three rat transporters that were confirmed to transport specific PFAS. We proposed a candidate list of seven untested kidney transporters with the potential for PFAS transport. Model results indicated PFOA toxicokinetics were more influenced by changes in GFR than in transporter expression.</p></sec><sec><title>Discussion:</title><p>Studies on additional transporters, particularly efflux transporters, and on more PFAS, especially current-use PFAS, are needed to better cover the role of transporters across the PFAS class. Remaining research gaps in transporter expression changes in specific kidney disease states could limit the effectiveness of risk assessment and prevent identification of vulnerable populations. <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11885" ext-link-type="uri">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11885</ext-link></p></sec></abstract><custom-meta-group><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-qastatus</meta-name><meta-value>0</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-live</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-embargo</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-released</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-open-access</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-olf</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-legally-suppressed</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-pdf</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-supplement</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-pdf-only</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-suppress-copyright</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-real-version</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-scanned-article</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-preprint</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-in-epmc</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta><fn-group><fn fn-type="other" specific-use="pdf-only"><p>Supplemental Material is available online (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11885" ext-link-type="uri">https://doi.org/10.1289/EHP11885</ext-link>).</p></fn><fn fn-type="financial-disclosure"><p>A.D. assisted with the design and implementation of the C8 Health Project. He has testified on behalf of attorneys for three communities with PFAS water contamination where residents have sought medical screening. The remaining authors declare they have nothing to disclose.</p></fn><fn specific-use="pdf-only" fn-type="other"><p><bold>Note to readers with disabilities:</bold>
<italic toggle="yes">EHP</italic> strives to ensure that all journal content is accessible to all readers. However, some figures and Supplemental Material published in <italic toggle="yes">EHP</italic> articles may not conform to <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="http://ehp.niehs.nih.gov/accessibility/" ext-link-type="uri">508 standards</ext-link> due to the complexity of the information being presented. If you need assistance accessing journal content, please contact <email>ehpsubmissions@niehs.nih.gov</email>. Our staff will work with you to assess and meet your accessibility needs within 3 working days.</p></fn></fn-group></front><body><sec id="s1"><title>Introduction</title><p>Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), a class of thousands of manufactured chemicals with at least one perfluorinated carbon atom, have been ubiquitously detected in the environment,<sup><xref rid="c1" ref-type="bibr">1</xref>,<xref rid="c2" ref-type="bibr">2</xref></sup> humans,<sup><xref rid="c3" ref-type="bibr">3</xref>–<xref rid="c5" ref-type="bibr">5</xref></sup> and wildlife<sup><xref rid="c5" ref-type="bibr">5</xref></sup> worldwide. Of concern, identified health outcomes in humans and animals associated with specific PFAS exposures include altered immune function,<sup><xref rid="c6" ref-type="bibr">6</xref></sup> liver injury,<sup><xref rid="c7" ref-type="bibr">7</xref>,<xref rid="c8" ref-type="bibr">8</xref></sup> kidney disease,<sup><xref rid="c9" ref-type="bibr">9</xref></sup> and adverse reproductive and developmental outcomes.<sup><xref rid="c10" ref-type="bibr">10</xref></sup> In humans and animals, absorbed PFAS were shown to undergo key physiological processes, such as enterohepatic circulation, including secretion into bile and eventual reabsorption into the cell by the intestine,<sup><xref rid="c11" ref-type="bibr">11</xref>,<xref rid="c12" ref-type="bibr">12</xref></sup> as well as kidney reabsorption and secretion.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> PFAS can be excreted from the body through urine and feces.<sup><xref rid="c14" ref-type="bibr">14</xref></sup> Previous studies have shown that PFAS bioaccumulation is related to their binding to serum albumin<sup><xref rid="c15" ref-type="bibr">15</xref>–<xref rid="c17" ref-type="bibr">17</xref></sup> and to fatty acid-binding proteins (FABPs)<sup><xref rid="c18" ref-type="bibr">18</xref></sup> and cell membrane transport<sup><xref rid="c19" ref-type="bibr">19</xref>–<xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref></sup> in various organs and tissues, including the liver, gut, kidney, and brain. High binding affinities to serum albumin<sup><xref rid="c23" ref-type="bibr">23</xref></sup> and FABPs<sup><xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref></sup> were observed for long-chain PFAS (e.g., C6–C11) compared with short-chain PFAS (e.g., <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i1" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c less than 6"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>).</p><p>For cell membrane transport, passive diffusion and protein-facilitated transport have been considered primary mechanisms for cellular uptake or secretion of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> Studies have identified a number of transport proteins located at the surface of endothelial cells responsible for PFAS cellular transport in humans<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> and rats.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> Throughout this review, transporters are named using all capital letters for human transporters or using one capital letter followed by lowercase letters for animal transporters.<sup><xref rid="c28" ref-type="bibr">28</xref></sup> Known PFAS transporters include organic anion transporters (OATs/Oats)<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> and organic anion-transporting polypeptides (OATPs/Oatps).<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> PFAS can bind to these transporters and achieve transmembrane transport (e.g., uptake into the cell or secretion from the cell) through the conformational changes of transporters.<sup><xref rid="c29" ref-type="bibr">29</xref></sup> Studies have shown that the contribution of passive diffusion to the total PFAS uptake in cells varies between <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i2" display="inline" alttext="approximately 5 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and 60%,<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> indicating that transporter-facilitated uptake is an important and often dominant component of PFAS transport regardless of cell type.</p><p>In animals, sex- and species-specific differences in elimination half-lives of PFAS have been linked to renal elimination, particularly the activity of kidney transport proteins and polypeptides for organic anions.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> Renal elimination of PFAS includes one passive diffusion process, namely glomerular filtration, and two active pathways, namely tubular secretion and reabsorption (<xref rid="f1" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>).<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> Secretion and reabsorption of PFAS in the kidney are mediated via renal transporters expressed on the basolateral (facing the blood) and the apical (facing the urine) membranes of the proximal tubular cells.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> Renal transporters can facilitate either uptake or efflux, or both under certain conditions.<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> An inverted U-shaped distribution has been reported between human serum PFAS concentrations and declining estimated glomerular filtration rates (eGFR), as eGFR changes from healthy kidney function through the advancing stages of kidney disease.<sup><xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref></sup> This inverted U-shaped pattern differs by sex.<sup><xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref></sup> One possible reason for this observed pattern may relate to the retained activities, during progressive kidney failure, of renal transporters that actively mediate PFAS secretion and reabsorption.<sup><xref rid="c33" ref-type="bibr">33</xref></sup> Another possible explanation is that early subclinical kidney damage can manifest initially as glomerular hyperfiltration as an adaptive response to nephron injury or loss, which may precede clinical functional decline.<sup><xref rid="c34" ref-type="bibr">34</xref>,<xref rid="c35" ref-type="bibr">35</xref></sup> The inverted U-shaped pattern could also be due to a combination of kidney disease causation due to PFAS (high PFAS concentration leading to progression of the kidney disease<sup><xref rid="c36" ref-type="bibr">36</xref></sup>) followed by enhanced excretion due to transporter failure in presence of albuminuria or moderate-to-severe declines in glomerular filtration.<sup><xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref></sup></p><fig position="float" id="f1" orientation="portrait"><label>Figure 1.</label><caption><p>Conceptual diagram of processes in renal PFAS clearance. Renal clearance of PFAS comprises glomerular filtration, tubular secretion, and tubular reabsorption. Note: PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances.</p></caption><alt-text>Figure 1 is an illustration, depicting the processes in renal per- and polyfluoroalkyl substance clearance. From urine, through reabsorption, uptake, and efflux inside the proximal tubular cell, to blood. From blood, through glomerular filtration, uptake and efflux inside the proximal tubular cell, and secretion to urine.</alt-text><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="ehp11885_f1.jpg"><?image-name ehp11885_f1.jpg?><?image-size 92275?><?image-md5 39e2ae270613a3372d3ff85555575afd?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 2469?><?image-original-width 2400?><?image-scaled-height 825?><?image-scaled-width 802?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/39e2ae270613/ehp11885_f1.jpg?><?thumb-name ehp11885_f1.gif?><?thumb-size 4092?><?thumb-md5 476109a5a16e65035c9d99499cd0669d?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 103?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/476109a5a16e/ehp11885_f1.gif?></graphic></fig><p>Previous studies have confirmed the role of renal transporters in facilitating PFAS transport in rats<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref>,<xref rid="c37" ref-type="bibr">37</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> and humans.<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> For example, Weaver et al. reported that Oat1 and Oat3 were involved in rat renal secretion of perfluoroheptanoic acid (PFHpA), perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA), and perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA), whereas rat renal Oatp1a1 reportedly could contribute to the reabsorption of PFOA, PFNA, and perfluorodecanoic acid (PFDA).<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> Nakagawa et al. concluded that both human and rat renal OAT1/Oat1 and OAT3/Oat3 facilitated PFOA transport to similar degrees, whereas neither human nor rat renal OAT2/Oat2 facilitated PFOA transport, indicating the difference between the PFOA half-lives in humans and rats is not likely attributable to differences in the affinities of these three renal transporters.<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> In contrast, OAT4 and urate transporter 1 (URAT1) were identified as key transporters in kidney reabsorption of PFOA in humans and, as a result, may contribute significantly to the long half-life of PFOA in humans.<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> However, these are a small subset of kidney transporters, considering the large number of kidney transporter superfamilies that exist<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> [i.e., the solute carrier (<italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>) and the adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette (<italic toggle="yes">ABC</italic>) gene families].</p><p>In addition, expression levels and functions of renal transporters can be altered in various kidney disease states, with disease-specific outcomes.<sup><xref rid="c40" ref-type="bibr">40</xref>–<xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> For example, expression levels of Oat1 were significantly decreased 24 h after ischemia/reperfusion–induced acute kidney injury (AKI) in rats, but returned to normal after 72 h.<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> Conversely, expression levels of Oat1 were not significantly changed during the first 2 wk following 5/6 nephrectomy (removal of one kidney first followed by two poles of the remaining kidney 1 wk later<sup><xref rid="c41" ref-type="bibr">41</xref></sup>) in rats<sup><xref rid="c44" ref-type="bibr">44</xref></sup> but were significantly decreased after 6 wk.<sup><xref rid="c45" ref-type="bibr">45</xref></sup> To the best of our knowledge, no previous studies have investigated the influence of altered renal transporter expression by kidney disease status on the renal elimination of PFAS.</p><p>Previous observational studies have shown long half-lives of long-chain PFAS (i.e., <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i3" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic greater than or equal to 6"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi><mml:mo>≥</mml:mo><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) in humans; for instance, mean human serum half-lives for PFOA (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i4" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 8 end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>8</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i5" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 8 end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>8</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), and perfluorohexane sulfonate (PFHxS; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i6" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 6 end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) ranged from <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i7" display="inline" alttext="approximately 2 to 3"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mtext> to </mml:mtext><mml:mn>3</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i8" display="inline" alttext="approximately 3 to 4"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>3</mml:mn><mml:mtext> to </mml:mtext><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i9" display="inline" alttext="approximately 5 to 7"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>5</mml:mn><mml:mtext> to </mml:mtext><mml:mn>7</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> y, respectively.<sup><xref rid="c46" ref-type="bibr">46</xref>–<xref rid="c51" ref-type="bibr">51</xref></sup> In contrast, shorter human serum half-lives were observed for short-chain PFAS (e.g., <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i10" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c less than 6"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>6</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), with a mean half-life for perfluorobutane sulfonic acid (PFBS; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i11" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 4 end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>4</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) of 44 d, and for perfluoropentane sulfonic acid (PFPeS; <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i12" display="inline" alttext="uppercase c begin subscript 5 end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">C</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>5</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) of 230 d.<sup><xref rid="c52" ref-type="bibr">52</xref></sup> Notably, an observational study by Li et al.<sup><xref rid="c50" ref-type="bibr">50</xref></sup> reported PFHxS half-lives were widely variable among 106 individuals, and the reason is still unknown. Sex differences were observed, with more rapid elimination in women for PFHxS and PFOS, but comparable elimination for PFOA.<sup><xref rid="c50" ref-type="bibr">50</xref></sup> Uncertainties in PFAS half-lives may exist because they are based on observations in groups with differing exposure patterns, such as retired fluorochemical workers and residents of contaminated communities with serum levels investigated after exposure via drinking water. These half-lives should be expected to differ from steady-state exposures, or even from declining PFAS background exposure, such as from food.<sup><xref rid="c50" ref-type="bibr">50</xref></sup> Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling can be ideal to address these questions and examine how renal transporter expression and changes in kidney function between healthy and diseased states could impact estimations of PFAS half-lives representing accumulation and elimination over time. Indeed, even in the case where PFAS exposure itself may instigate kidney disease,<sup><xref rid="c9" ref-type="bibr">9</xref>,<xref rid="c32" ref-type="bibr">32</xref>,<xref rid="c53" ref-type="bibr">53</xref></sup> if the impact of that disease on transporters is known, it can also be integrated into the PBPK modeling of changes in PFAS toxicokinetics.</p><p>This review aimed to address how kidney function and the related expression of key transporters could affect PFAS toxicokinetics by facilitating their elimination and reabsorption. We first surveyed studies related to kidney transporters that mediate PFAS transport to summarize current knowledge. We then conducted a broader survey of other kidney transporters that could have the potential for molecular interactions with PFAS based on the similarity between PFAS and the transporters’ endogenous ligands. Additional literature was reviewed to provide existing knowledge about kidney transport proteins or mRNA expression levels based on known changes in characteristic disease states. Finally, we reviewed studies that developed PBPK models for PFAS and used the model developed by Cheng and Ng<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> to illustrate how changes in renal transporter expression levels, GFR, and serum albumin in specific kidney disease states could impact PFAS toxicokinetics. We highlighted key insights into population and interindividual variability in contaminant toxicokinetics that are critical to identifying particularly sensitive populations for public health interventions.</p></sec><sec id="s2"><title>Methods</title><sec id="s2.1"><title>Literature Search</title><p>We performed a literature review using the Web of Science, Scopus, and PubMed databases to locate studies focusing on PFAS, renal transporters, and PBPK models. We searched articles through 15 April 2023 with no lower limit on publication date, using the following search terms: <italic toggle="yes">a</italic>) for PFAS interactions with renal transporters, the search terms were (“PFAS” OR “perfluoro” OR “PFOA” OR “PFOS” AND “renal transporters” OR “kidney” OR “drug transporters”); <italic toggle="yes">b</italic>) for expression-level changes of renal transporters in kidney disease, the search terms were (“renal transporters” OR “drug transporters” and “kidney disease” OR “kidney failure” OR “AKI” OR “CKD”); and <italic toggle="yes">c</italic>) for PFAS-related PBPK models, the search terms were (“PFAS” OR “perfluoro” OR “PFOA” OR “PFOS” AND “PBPK models”). Four terms for PFAS (i.e., PFAS, perfluoro, PFOA, and PFOS) and three terms for renal transporter (i.e., renal transporter, kidney, and drug transporter) were included to reduce the possibility of missing a potential study that fulfilled our inclusion criteria. We also used the Research Rabbit application (version 32.2.0), a citation-based literature mapping tool, for an additional search. Specifically, we imported the articles identified from Web of Science, Scopus, and PubMed databases and used Research Rabbit to identify other relevant articles; we then reviewed these articles to identify further publications related to our research questions.</p><p>We conducted a search to create a candidate list of untested renal transporters with the potential to transport PFAS. Specifically, we first used a website-based transporter database included in the Guide to PHARMACOLOGY database<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.guidetopharmacology.org" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.guidetopharmacology.org</ext-link>; last accessed 12 April 2023) to identify transporters known to have fatty acids, bile acids, and phospholipids as substrates. We then conducted a literature search via Web of Science, Scopus, and PubMed for articles reporting transporters in tissues other than kidney that transport PFAS using the search terms (“PFAS” OR “perfluoro” OR “PFOA” OR “PFOS” AND “transporters” OR “transporting polypeptide” OR “drug transporters”). We excluded transporters already identified to transport PFAS in the kidney, as summarized in <xref rid="t1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>. After selecting the potential transporters, we used the Human Protein Atlas database<sup><xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.proteinatlas.org" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.proteinatlas.org</ext-link>; last accessed 12 April 2023) to confirm their expression in the kidney. Given that resources for animal transporters were relatively limited, we included only human transporters in the candidate list.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="t1" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 1</label><caption><p>Tissue distributions, sex and species differences of tested human, rat, and zebrafish renal transporters for PFAS.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><colgroup span="1"><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/></colgroup><thead><tr><th align="left" id="t1c1" scope="col" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Gene name</th><th align="left" id="t1c2" scope="col" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Protein name</th><th align="left" id="t1c3" scope="col" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Tissue distribution<xref rid="TF2" ref-type="table-fn"><sup><italic toggle="yes">a</italic></sup></xref></th><th align="left" id="t1c4" scope="col" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Sex difference<xref rid="TF3" ref-type="table-fn"><sup><italic toggle="yes">b</italic></sup></xref></th><th align="left" id="t1c5" scope="col" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Species difference between human and rat<xref rid="TF3" ref-type="table-fn"><sup><italic toggle="yes">b</italic></sup></xref></th></tr></thead><tbody valign="top"><tr><th colspan="5" align="left" id="t1c6" scope="row" headers="t1c1" rowspan="1">Human (source for tissue distribution: <ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.proteinatlas.org" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.proteinatlas.org</ext-link>)</th></tr><tr><td align="left" id="t1c7" scope="row" headers="t1c1 t1c6" colspan="1" rowspan="1">

<italic toggle="yes"> SLCO1D1</italic>
</td><td align="left" headers="t1c2 t1c26" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Oatp1d1</td><td align="left" headers="t1c3 t1c26" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Intestine, liver, brain, gills, muscle, kidney, and ovary</td><td align="center" headers="t1c4 t1c26" colspan="1" rowspan="1">—</td><td align="left" headers="t1c5 t1c26" colspan="1" rowspan="1">OATP1A</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><fn id="TF1"><p>Note: —, no information available; ABC, adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette gene family; ASBT/Asbt, apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter; GI, gastrointestinal; MRP/Mrp, multidrug resistance proteins; NPT/Npt, sodium–phosphate transporters; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; OATP/Oatp, organic anion-transporting polypeptides; OST, organic solute transporter; PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; SLC, solute carrier gene family; URAT/Urat, urate transporter.</p></fn><fn id="TF2"><label>

<sup>
<italic toggle="yes">c</italic>

</label><p>Transporter only has mRNA expression in that tissue according to the database.</p></fn><fn id="TF5"><label>
<sup>
<italic toggle="yes">d</italic>
</sup>
</label><p>“Broad distribution”: found in brain, eye, endocrine tissues, lung, proximal digestive tract, GI tract, liver and gallbladder, pancreas, kidney and urinary bladder, reproductive tissues, muscle, adipose, skin, and bone.<sup><xref rid="c24" ref-type="bibr">24</xref>,<xref rid="c36" ref-type="bibr">36</xref></sup></p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></sec><sec id="s2.2"><title>Study Selection</title><p>All articles underwent an identical screening process with specified inclusion and exclusion criteria. The inclusion criteria were established based on the research question of interest, which focused on how renal transporter expression and changes in health and disease state could impact PFAS half-life estimates. Regardless of the type (e.g., human observational studies or <italic toggle="yes">in vivo, ex vivo, in vitro</italic> studies), studies were included if they <italic toggle="yes">a</italic>) assessed the direct interaction between transporters and PFAS, <italic toggle="yes">b</italic>) quantified the expression and changes of renal transporters in kidney diseases, or <italic toggle="yes">c</italic>) developed PBPK models for PFAS. The exclusion criteria were that the studies <italic toggle="yes">a</italic>) indirectly assessed the influence of renal transporters on PFAS (e.g., renal transporters inhibited by certain PFAS), <italic toggle="yes">b</italic>) did not quantify the changes in renal transporter expression levels during kidney diseases, or <italic toggle="yes">c</italic>) developed PBPK models for chemicals other than PFAS.</p><p>Titles and abstracts of identified studies were scanned for eligibility by two reviewers, with any discrepancies resolved by a third reviewer. All full texts in English were then reviewed by two reviewers for final inclusion in the qualitative summary using the same inclusion and exclusion criteria, with a third reviewer again resolving discrepancies. The literature was complemented by identifying additional articles by analyzing the reference lists in various review articles.</p></sec><sec id="s2.3"><title>Data Extraction</title><p>The following contents were extracted from the identified articles: <italic toggle="yes">a</italic>) for PFAS interaction with renal transporters, renal transporter characteristics (type, localization, and function), PFAS (specific compounds tested), uptake pattern, and information on the methods used; <italic toggle="yes">b</italic>) for expression-level changes of renal transporters as a result of kidney disease, renal transporter characteristics (type, localization, and function), changes in expression levels, the type of kidney disease, and information on the methods (e.g., <italic toggle="yes">in vivo</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">ex vivo</italic>, <italic toggle="yes">in vitro</italic>, or clinical); <italic toggle="yes">c</italic>) for PFAS transporters expressed in tissues other than kidney, transporter characteristics (type, localization, and function); and <italic toggle="yes">d</italic>) for PFAS-PBPK models, information on whether the models included transporter-specific mechanisms.</p></sec><sec id="s2.4"><title>Using an Existing Model to Explore the Influence of Kidney Disease on PFOA Toxicokinetics</title><p>We used a PFOA-PBPK model for male rats, developed by Cheng and Ng<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> in Python (version 3.10.8) to explore the influence of changes in transporter expression levels, GFR, and serum albumin concentration on PFOA toxicokinetics. Details on the model development can be found in Cheng and Ng<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup>; an overview of the model is provided here. The Cheng and Ng model is a permeability-limited PBPK model that includes four kidney transporters [i.e., Oat 1, Oat 3, Oatp1a1, and organic solute transporter-alpha/beta (Ost <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i14" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>)], glomerular filtration using a parameter for GFR, and binding to serum albumin as mechanisms for PFOA toxicokinetics.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> To model changes in the toxicokinetics in response to kidney disease, we reviewed reported changes from other published studies. Schneider et al.<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> reported that GFR was decreased to 5%, 15%, and 33% of levels in sham-operated rats in 6, 24, and 72 h, respectively, after ischemia/reperfusion–induced AKI.</p><p>To model the potential impacts of kidney disease, we made the following adjustments to the Cheng and Ng model parameterization described in the original paper, we set <italic toggle="yes">a</italic>) the clearance rate constant for Oat1 and Oat3 to 20% of the original model default value to simulate how changes in transporter levels during kidney disease could affect PFOA toxicokinetics in the male rat, <italic toggle="yes">b</italic>) GFR to 33% and 5% of the original model default to simulate how changes in GFR during kidney disease could affect PFOA toxicokinetics, and <italic toggle="yes">c</italic>) albumin concentration to 70% of the original model default value to simulate the influence of albumin decrease owing to albuminuria. For transporter levels, the changes of Oat1 and Oat3 expression at 6 wk in <xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">Figure 4A</xref> were used to represent the worst-case scenario.</p></sec></sec><sec id="s3"><title>Results</title><sec id="s3.1"><title>Current Knowledge of Renal Transporters for PFAS Transport</title><p>We identified 508 papers based on our search terms and procedures. After exclusion of 498 papers because they did not report PFAS transport by renal transporters, 10 studies that directly assessed the uptake of PFAS by renal transporters were included in this review (Excel Table S1).</p><p>Perfluoroalkyl acids (PFAAs) were the most commonly investigated PFAS for their interaction with renal transporters.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref>,<xref rid="c37" ref-type="bibr">37</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> Given that PFAAs are ionic compounds that are negatively charged when in aqueous environments (e.g., human fluids), renal transporters that have the ability to transport organic anions, such as OATs/Oats and URAT1/Urat1 from the <italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>22 gene family and OATP1A1/Oatp1a1 from the <italic toggle="yes">SLCO</italic> gene family, were among the first to be investigated for their potential PFAS transport. To the best of our knowledge, the first-reported direct investigation of PFAS transport by renal transporters was by Katakura et al., who confirmed that rat renal Oat3 (an uptake transporter located at the basolateral membrane facing blood) and Oatp1a1 (an uptake transporter located at the apical membrane facing urine) facilitated PFOA transport, whereas Npt2 (type II sodium-dependent phosphate transporter, an uptake transporter located at the apical membrane facing urine) did not.<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> An earlier study by Kudo et al.<sup><xref rid="c37" ref-type="bibr">37</xref></sup> provided indirect evidence for renal Oats (Oat2 and Oat3) facilitating PFOA transport by multiple regression analysis between plasma PFOA concentrations and Oats mRNA levels in rats. Subsequent studies of PFAS transport by renal transporters have accumulated, with 10 additional original research studies published since 2007.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref>,<xref rid="c57" ref-type="bibr">57</xref>,<xref rid="c58" ref-type="bibr">58</xref></sup> Four gene families other than <italic toggle="yes">SLC22</italic><sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref>,<xref rid="c57" ref-type="bibr">57</xref></sup> and <italic toggle="yes">SLCO</italic><sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> were investigated for their role in PFAS transport, including <italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>10 (sodium–bile acid cotransporters),<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup>
<italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>34 (sodium–phosphate cotransporters),<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup>
<italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>51 (steroid-derived molecule transporters),<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> and <italic toggle="yes">ABCC</italic> (one of the five families in the ABC superfamily).<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup></p><p>A review by Han et al.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> summarized the knowledge of renal transporters for PFAS transport prior to 2011. In this subsection, we update that knowledge with four additionally identified renal transporters, specify the PFAS investigated to date, and expand information on the distribution of these transporters in other tissues, as well as whether the transporter could be responsible for sex or species differences (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref> and <xref rid="t1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>) by reviewing identified studies (<xref rid="t2" ref-type="table">Table 2</xref>). In this review, the renal transporters investigated in previous studies were classified according to their expression localizations [i.e., basolateral (facing the blood) or apical membrane (facing the urine) of the proximal tubular cells] and their cellular transport roles (i.e., cellular uptake or cellular efflux). This grouping deliberately oversimplifies the complex processes of renal elimination; however, it helps summarize the mechanisms of PFAS renal transport. Specifically, the uptake role of transporters at the basolateral membrane and the efflux role of transporters at the apical membrane contribute to PFAS secretion (i.e., negative contribution to PFAS reabsorption). In contrast, the uptake role of transporters at the apical membrane and the efflux role of transporters at the basolateral membrane have negative contribution to PFAS secretion (i.e., positive contribution to PFAS reabsorption).</p><fig position="float" id="f2" orientation="portrait"><label>Figure 2.</label><caption><p>(A) Human and (B) rat renal transporters that have been tested for PFAS transport summarized from previous studies using <italic toggle="yes">in vitro</italic> methods. *OATP2B1 was identified only by mRNA expression in kidney according to the Human Protein Atlas database.<sup><xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> Transporter-facilitated uptake was often the dominant component of PFAS transport given that contribution of passive diffusion to the total PFAS uptake in cells varies between 5% and 60%.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>–<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c31" ref-type="bibr">31</xref></sup> PFAS denoted by strikethrough text indicates that compound was evaluated but reported to not be transported by the transporter. PFAS superscripts denote literature sources, and details are provided in <xref rid="t2" ref-type="table">Table 2</xref>. Note: PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; PFBS, perfluorobutane sulfonic acid; PFDA, perfluorodecanoic acid; PFHpA, perfluoroheptanoic acid; PFHxS, perfluorohexane sulfonate; PFNA, perfluorononanoic acid; PFOA, perfluorooctanoic acid; PFOS, perfluorooctane sulfonate.</p></caption><alt-text>Figure 2A is an illustration, depicting a human proximal tubular cell. <italic toggle="yes">In vitro</italic>
</td><td align="left" headers="t2c4 t2c14" colspan="1" rowspan="1">CHO</td><td align="left" headers="t2c5 t2c14" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Oatp1a1</td><td align="left" headers="t2c6 t2c14" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PFOA</td></tr><tr><td align="left" id="t2c15" scope="row" headers="t2c1" colspan="1" rowspan="1">9</td><td align="left" headers="t2c2 t2c15" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Louisse et al. 2023<sup><xref rid="c57" ref-type="bibr">57</xref></sup></td><td align="center" headers="t2c3 t2c15" colspan="1" rowspan="1">
<italic toggle="yes">In vitro</italic>
</td><td align="left" headers="t2c4 t2c15" colspan="1" rowspan="1">HEK293</td><td align="left" headers="t2c5 t2c15" colspan="1" rowspan="1">OAT4</td><td align="left" headers="t2c6 t2c15" colspan="1" rowspan="1">PFCAs (C7–C10); PFBS; PFHxS; PFOS</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><fn id="TF6"><p>Note: See <xref rid="t1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref> for the protein names. ASBT/Asbt, apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter; C, carbon; CHO, Chinese hamster ovarian; HEK293, human embryonic kidney 293; MRP/Mrp, multidrug resistance proteins; NA, not available; NPT/Npt, sodium–phosphate transporters; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; OATP/Oatp, organic anion-transporting polypeptides; PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; PFBS, perfluorobutane sulfonic acid; PFCAs, perfluoroalkyl carboxylic acids; PFHxS, perfluorohexane sulfonic acid; PFOA, perfluorooctanoic acid; PFOS, perfluorooctane sulfonic acid; URAT/Urat, urate transporter.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><p>So far, nine human renal uptake transporters have been investigated for their potential importance to PFAS transport (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2A</xref>). These are: OAT1,<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> OAT2,<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> OAT3,<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> OAT4,<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> URAT1,<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> OATP1A2,<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> OATP2B1,<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref></sup> apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter (ASBT),<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> and OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i15" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> The expression of most of these nine transporters (except for OATP2B1) in kidney was indicated by protein expression, whereas, to the best of our knowledge, at the time of this review only mRNA expression in kidney had been demonstrated for OATP2B1,<sup><xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> suggesting expression of OATP2B1 protein in kidney needs to be confirmed. OAT2<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> and OATP1A2<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> are the two transporters that did not exhibit significant activity in PFOA transport. To date, five transporters (namely OAT1, OAT3, OAT4, URAT1, and OATP2B1) have been confirmed to transport PFOA. In addition, ASBT and OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i16" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> reportedly mediated transport of PFOS, and OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i17" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> also facilitated PFBS and PFHxS transport, but ASBT did not; to the best of our knowledge, the role of ASBT and OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i18" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> has not been tested in PFOA transport. OATP2B1 and OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i19" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> have been shown to transport multiple PFAS (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2A</xref>) and longer-chain PFAS showed higher affinities for both transporters (order of affinity: <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i20" display="inline" alttext="uppercase p f o s greater than uppercase p f h lowercase x uppercase s greater than uppercase p f b s"><mml:mrow><mml:mtext>PFOS</mml:mtext><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>PFHxS</mml:mtext><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mtext>PFBS</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>).<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref>,<xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref></sup> This trend aligns with observations of longer half-lives for longer-chain PFAS in humans.<sup><xref rid="c52" ref-type="bibr">52</xref></sup> Among the confirmed PFAS renal uptake transporters, OAT1 and OAT3 reside in the basolateral membrane of the proximal tubular cells and therefore participate in PFAS renal secretion.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> In contrast, OAT4, URAT1, and OATP2B1 are involved in PFAS renal reabsorption because they are expressed in the apical membrane of proximal tubular cells.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> To the best of our knowledge, OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i21" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, expressed in the basolateral membrane (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2A</xref>), was the only human renal efflux transporter that was tested and shown to transport PFBS, PFHxS, and PFOS,<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> indicating it was involved in renal reabsorption of these PFAS. Moreover, OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i22" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was shown to transport bidirectionally, depending on the concentration gradient of PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup></p><p>In the rat, eight renal uptake transporters have been studied for their potential role in PFAS transport; five of those did not transport PFAS (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2B</xref>). Specifically, rat renal Oat2 and Urat1 did not demonstrate the capability to transport PFHpA, PFOA, PFNA or PFDA,<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> Npt2 was not involved in PFOA transport,<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> Asbt did not transport PFBS, PFHxS, or PFOS,<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> and multidrug resistance protein 2 (Mrp2) did not facilitate PFOA transport.<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> In contrast, rat renal Oat1<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> and Oatp1a1 (also referred to as Oatp1)<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> were considered to facilitate transport for PFHpA, PFOA, PFNA, and PFDA with compound-specific activities, and Oat3 was reported to transport PFOA.<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref>,<xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> The affinities of PFAS with renal transporters showed transporter-specific patterns. For example, PFHpA and PFOA had the highest affinities for rat Oat1 and Oat3, but the transport efficiencies of PFHpA, PFOA, and PFNA by both Oat1 and Oat 3 were similar.<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> In contrast, PFNA and PFDA had higher affinities and efficiencies for Oatp1a1 compared with PFOA.<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> This trend also aligned with observations of longer half-lives for longer-chain PFAS in rats. In addition, rat renal uptake transporters Oat1 and Oat3 are expressed at the basolateral membrane, indicating that they positively contribute to PFAS renal secretion, whereas Oatp1a1 resides in the apical membrane, suggesting it is involved in PFAS renal reabsorption.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> To the best of our knowledge, Mrp2 is the only renal efflux transporter in rats that has been investigated for PFAS uptake, and a single previous study showed that Mrp2 did not play a role in PFOA renal elimination.<sup><xref rid="c30" ref-type="bibr">30</xref></sup> To date, one zebrafish renal transporter, Oatp1d1 (considered functionally similar to mammalian OATP1A/Oatp1a and OAT1B/Oatp1b), was investigated for its potential for PFAS transport mediation and showed the ability to mediate transport of PFOS but not PFOA.<sup><xref rid="c59" ref-type="bibr">59</xref></sup></p><p>OAT4 is a human-specific transporter<sup><xref rid="c60" ref-type="bibr">60</xref></sup> that was previously demonstrated to transport PFOA<sup><xref rid="c25" ref-type="bibr">25</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup>; thus, it may be one of the transporters responsible for species-specific differences in PFAS toxicokinetics. Human renal ASBT is another recently identified transporter that was considered responsible for species-specific differences in PFAS toxicokinetics based on findings by Zhao et al.<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> that human ASBT transported PFOS, whereas rat Asbt did not. Although the expression of human renal OAT2 and rat renal Oat2 are at the opposite sides of the proximal tubular cell, these transporters have not been identified as related to species-specific differences in PFAS toxicokinetics given that they have not demonstrated the ability to facilitate PFAS secretion/reabsorption. Human and rat OAT1/Oat1 and OAT3/Oat3 are considered to play a limited role in species-specific differences given that they have shown comparable uptake ability for PFAS in laboratory studies.<sup><xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref></sup> However, inferential human population evidence has suggested that OAT1 and OAT3 may operate differently in humans compared with rats. Inhibition of transporters (e.g., Oat1, Oat3) by probenecid had a marked and sex-differential effect on PFOA elimination in rats.<sup><xref rid="c61" ref-type="bibr">61</xref></sup> In contrast, probenecid had no discernible effect on PFOA or PFOS excretion in a human population.<sup><xref rid="c62" ref-type="bibr">62</xref></sup> The authors concluded that OAT1 and OAT3 were “lazy” PFAS excreters (having lower capability for PFAS excretion) in humans compared with rats.<sup><xref rid="c62" ref-type="bibr">62</xref></sup> However, another possible reason causing the difference in probenecid effects on rats and humans could be the species-specific mechanisms of reabsorption of PFOA. Probenecid is a known inhibitor of not only OATs/Oats,<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> but also OATPs/Oatps,<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> MRPs/MRPs,<sup><xref rid="c63" ref-type="bibr">63</xref></sup> and URAT1/Urat1.<sup><xref rid="c64" ref-type="bibr">64</xref></sup> OAT4 and URAT1 are responsible for PFOA reabsorption in humans, but rats do not have an Oat4 and rat Urat1 does not transport PFOA<sup><xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>). This suggests the reabsorption of PFOA could be inhibited by probenecid in humans but not in the rat and that URAT1 could also be responsible for species differences in PFOA elimination. Although sex differences in PFHxS and PFOS (but not PFOA) elimination were observed in humans,<sup><xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> to the best of our knowledge, no human renal transporters to date have been specifically identified as responsible. Sex differences in PFAS half-lives could be partially or mostly attributed to nonrenal causes, including menstruation, breast-feeding, and transplacental transport of PFAS from mother to fetus in humans.<sup><xref rid="c65" ref-type="bibr">65</xref>,<xref rid="c66" ref-type="bibr">66</xref></sup></p><p>Currently known PFAS renal transporters are also distributed in other tissues. For example, the human uptake transporter OAT4 is also expressed in the placenta,<sup><xref rid="c67" ref-type="bibr">67</xref></sup> indicating this transporter could play an important role in PFAS transfer from mother to fetus. OAT1 and OAT3 are also expressed in the brain, endocrine tissues, and gastrointestinal tract<sup><xref rid="c56" ref-type="bibr">56</xref></sup> (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.proteinatlas.org" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.proteinatlas.org</ext-link>; <xref rid="t1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>), indicating they may play multiple roles in PFAS distribution in the human body. OATPs are expressed in the brain, liver, lungs, kidney, testes, and intestines.<sup><xref rid="c68" ref-type="bibr">68</xref></sup> OATP2B1 is present in a variety of tissues, especially gut and liver, and including proximal tubules and basolateral epithelial cells of the kidney.<sup><xref rid="c69" ref-type="bibr">69</xref></sup>
<italic toggle="yes">In vitro</italic> evidence from the human colon cancer–derived Caco-2 cell line has suggested that OATP2B1 could be a contributor to the long human half-lives of several PFAS.<sup><xref rid="c70" ref-type="bibr">70</xref></sup> OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i23" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, the efflux transporter studied among the human renal transporters, also resides in the liver, gallbladder, and ileum, suggesting it may play a significant role in PFAS elimination through enterohepatic circulation. In addition, Oatp1d1 is also expressed in zebrafish liver, brain, intestine, gill, ovary, and muscle tissues.<sup><xref rid="c55" ref-type="bibr">55</xref></sup> Overall, more information is available on tissue distribution of human transporters compared with rat and zebrafish, complicating comparisons of species differences.</p></sec><sec id="s3.2"><title>Other Renal Transporters with the Potential for PFAS Transport</title><p>The currently known PFAS renal transporters are members of two transporter families: the SLC and ABC superfamilies.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> The SLC superfamily is the second largest family of membrane proteins after G protein–coupled receptors.<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> SLC transporters do not rely on ATP hydrolysis and are largely, but not exclusively, uptake transporters; that is, they generally facilitate cellular uptake of molecules (e.g., endogenous metabolites, drugs, toxicants).<sup><xref rid="c68" ref-type="bibr">68</xref>,<xref rid="c71" ref-type="bibr">71</xref>–<xref rid="c73" ref-type="bibr">73</xref></sup> ABC transporters are ubiquitous membrane proteins that use ATP hydrolysis and mostly function as efflux transporters.<sup><xref rid="c74" ref-type="bibr">74</xref>,<xref rid="c75" ref-type="bibr">75</xref></sup> A total of 48 families in the SLC superfamily and 5 families in the ABC superfamily have been identified based on sequence similarities; details about the families and transporters can be found in the Guide to PHARMACOLOGY database.<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> Notably, many transporters have overlaps in terms of their substrates; this happens among transporters in the same superfamily and also among those across the SLC and ABC superfamilies.<sup><xref rid="c74" ref-type="bibr">74</xref></sup></p><p>Kidney transporters with substrates that have similar molecular structures as PFAS or that have expression levels that were shown to positively correlate with PFAS concentrations were considered to have the potential for PFAS transport for this review. Fatty acids, which are endogenous substrates of FABPs, have similar structures as PFAS, especially perfluoroalkyl carboxylic acids.<sup><xref rid="c76" ref-type="bibr">76</xref></sup> A significant positive association was identified in humans between plasma PFOA and PFOS concentrations and bile acid levels.<sup><xref rid="c12" ref-type="bibr">12</xref></sup> In addition, phospholipid levels were significantly associated with long-chain PFAS concentrations in North Atlantic pilot whale tissues.<sup><xref rid="c77" ref-type="bibr">77</xref></sup> Based on our literature analysis, we proposed seven human renal transporters that may have the potential for PFAS transport (<xref rid="t3" ref-type="table">Table 3</xref>), suggesting these should be prioritized for further experimental evaluation. Specifically, OATP4A1, an uptake transporter at the basolateral membrane, was considered because of its ability to transport bile acids. MRP1, an efflux transporter at the basolateral membrane and MDR1/P-glycoprotein (MDR1/P-gp), an efflux transporter at the apical membrane, were also included because of their ability to transport phospholipids. Another efflux transporter at the apical membrane, breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP, also known as ABCP), was proposed because it has been shown to transport PFOA across the blood–testis barrier.<sup><xref rid="c84" ref-type="bibr">84</xref></sup> Three additional ABC transporters, namely, adrenoleukodystrophy protein (ALDP), ALD-related gene (ALDR), and peroxisomal membrane protein (PMP70) were included because they have been shown to transport very-long-chain fatty acids, monounsaturated very-long-chain fatty acids, branched-chain fatty acids and bile acids.<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> Unfortunately, to the best of our knowledge, information on the localization and mechanisms (uptake or efflux) is not currently available for these three transporters.</p><table-wrap position="float" id="t3" orientation="portrait"><label>Table 3</label><caption><p>Candidate list of human renal transporters that have not been tested but may have potential for PFAS transport.</p></caption><table frame="hsides" rules="groups"><colgroup span="1"><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" valign="top" span="1"/><col align="left" See <xref rid="t1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref> for other protein names. —, No information available; ABC, adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding cassette gene family; ABCP/BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; ASBT/Asbt, apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter; ALDP, adrenoleukodystrophy protein; ALDR, ALD-related gene; MDR/MRP, multidrug resistance protein; OATP/Oatp, organic anion-transporting polypeptides; PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; PFOA, perfluorooctanoic acid; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; PMP, peroxisomal membrane protein; SLC, solute carrier gene family.</p></fn><fn id="TF8"><label>
<sup>
<italic toggle="yes">a</italic>
</sup>
</label><p>Website-based Guide to PHARMACOLOGY database<sup><xref rid="c39" ref-type="bibr">39</xref></sup> (<ext-link xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="https://www.guidetopharmacology.org" ext-link-type="uri">https://www.guidetopharmacology.org</ext-link>).</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap></sec><sec id="s3.3"><title>Levels and Patterns of Renal Transporter Expression in Kidney Disease</title><p>We identified 2,773 papers from the search for transporter expression-level changes in kidney disease (Excel Table S2). After exclusion of 2,749 papers, a total of 24 studies that quantified the changes of renal transporter expression during kidney disease were identified. Among these 24 publications, 12 studies used animal models to investigate renal transporter-level changes during AKI, 8 studies used animal models to investigate renal transporter changes during CKD, 3 used animal models to investigate renal transporter changes during diabetic nephropathy, and 1 assessed renal transporter changes in clinical patients with renal diseases (Excel Table S2).</p><p>Given the important role of relevant renal transporters in PFAS elimination, changes in expression levels and patterns of these transporters with kidney disease could substantially influence PFAS biological half-lives for specific populations. Kidney diseases can be acute or chronic, and both types can cause changes in transporter expressions and GFR. AKI is defined as acute renal dysfunction with mild-to-severe changes in renal function.<sup><xref rid="c85" ref-type="bibr">85</xref>,<xref rid="c86" ref-type="bibr">86</xref></sup> Chronic kidney disease (CKD) is defined as GFR <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i24" display="inline" alttext="less than 60 milliliters per minute per 1.73 meters squared"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>60</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>min per </mml:mtext><mml:mn>1.73</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of functioning nephrons or markers of kidney damage (e.g., nephrotic syndrome, urinary tract symptoms), or both, of at least 3-months duration.<sup><xref rid="c87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref></sup> CKD is classified into five stages based on GFR. Stages 1 and 2 are defined as normal and mild kidney damage, respectively, stages 3 to 4 are defined as moderate and severe kidney damage, and stage 5 (GFR <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i25" display="inline" alttext="less than 15 milliliters per minute per 1.73 meters squared"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>15</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>min per </mml:mtext><mml:mn>1.73</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) is defined as kidney failure, also referred to as end-stage renal disease.<sup><xref rid="c88" ref-type="bibr">88</xref></sup> Although the causes of CKD vary globally, diabetes and hypertension are the main causes of CKD in all high- and middle-income counties, as well as in many low-income countries, whereas glomerulonephritis is another important cause of CKD, especially in Asia and sub-Saharan Africa.<sup><xref rid="c87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref></sup> Environmental exposures to metals and organic compounds have also been implicated in CKD epidemics,<sup><xref rid="c87" ref-type="bibr">87</xref>,<xref rid="c89" ref-type="bibr">89</xref>,<xref rid="c90" ref-type="bibr">90</xref></sup> and epidemiologic relationships have been identified between toxic metals, such as lead, and organic compounds, such as PFAS, with CKD.<sup><xref rid="c9" ref-type="bibr">9</xref>,<xref rid="c91" ref-type="bibr">91</xref></sup> In addition, albuminuria is an indicator of CKD and could also be a pathway of PFAS excretion given that many PFAS have been shown to bind to serum albumin.<sup><xref rid="c15" ref-type="bibr">15</xref>–<xref rid="c17" ref-type="bibr">17</xref></sup> The impact of albuminuria on PFAS toxicokinetics is discussed in the subsequent PBPK modeling section.</p><p>Previous studies have demonstrated that renal transporter expression levels were altered by the presence of kidney disease, with transporter- and disease-specific outcomes in rats,<sup><xref rid="c45" ref-type="bibr">45</xref>,<xref rid="c92" ref-type="bibr">92</xref>–<xref rid="c97" ref-type="bibr">97</xref></sup> and humans.<sup><xref rid="c98" ref-type="bibr">98</xref>,<xref rid="c99" ref-type="bibr">99</xref></sup> On the other hand, changes in renal transporter expression levels and function could also lead to kidney disease progression.<sup><xref rid="c100" ref-type="bibr">100</xref>–<xref rid="c103" ref-type="bibr">103</xref></sup> The changes that may occur in renal transporters because of different kidney disease pathologies or over the development of disease are not fully understood owing to the complexity of kidney diseases and the fact that they often occur alongside other comorbidities, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and cardiovascular disease.<sup><xref rid="c104" ref-type="bibr">104</xref>–<xref rid="c107" ref-type="bibr">107</xref></sup> Nevertheless, we summarized the current knowledge on changes in the expression levels of renal transporters that have the ability or the potential for PFAS transport according to reported specified kidney diseases.</p><p>Most of the available information was derived from animal models and <italic toggle="yes">in vitro</italic> approaches. Human evidence (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3A</xref>) included significantly lower BCRP expression levels in patients with glomerulonephritis secondary to small vessel vasculitis (SVV) and in P-gp expression levels in patients with glomerulonephritis secondary to systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE).<sup><xref rid="c108" ref-type="bibr">108</xref></sup> Nonsignificant differences were observed for other transporters (i.e., OATP1A2 and MRP2) in different patient groups (i.e., SVV, SVV-control, SLE, and SLE-control, <xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3A</xref>).<sup><xref rid="c108" ref-type="bibr">108</xref></sup> An <italic toggle="yes">ex vivo</italic> study found that expression levels of P-gp in Sprague-Dawley female rats were <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i26" display="inline" alttext="approximately 260 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>260</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i27" display="inline" alttext="approximately 160 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>160</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> higher compared with levels in control rats 4 wk after uninephrectomy and 2 wk after uninephrectomy and diabetes, respectively (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3B</xref>).<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> Six research articles were selected for reporting the time-dependent expression-level differences in renal transporter expression in rats with experimental drug (e.g., lycopene, cisplatin)–induced AKI or ischemia/reperfusion–induced AKI.<sup><xref rid="c40" ref-type="bibr">40</xref>,<xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref>,<xref rid="c109" ref-type="bibr">109</xref>–<xref rid="c112" ref-type="bibr">112</xref></sup> After compiling the data for each transporter from different articles (articles 1–11 in <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref>), we found a U-shaped distribution for Oat1 and Oat3 expression levels as a function of time, with either lower expression or no difference in expression compared with the levels of controls (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3C</xref>). Oatp1a1 expression levels were 20% of control levels in 96 h after cisplatin-induced AKI (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3C</xref>),<sup><xref rid="c114" ref-type="bibr">114</xref></sup> whereas Mrp2 expression levels were 220% and 280% of control levels in 96 h<sup><xref rid="c114" ref-type="bibr">114</xref></sup> and 240 h,<sup><xref rid="c109" ref-type="bibr">109</xref></sup> respectively, after cisplatin-induced AKI (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3D</xref>). Expression levels of Mrp1 and P-gp also were higher—180% and 600% of control levels in 96 h after cisplatin-induced AKI.<sup><xref rid="c114" ref-type="bibr">114</xref></sup> More renal transporters were investigated in experimental CKD than AKI using rat models. Similar to the distribution in AKI, Oat1 and Oat3 expression levels in rats showed a U-shaped pattern with time after 5/6 nephrectomy (<xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">Figure 4A</xref>) based on data compiled from literature (articles 12–19 in <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref>).<sup><xref rid="c41" ref-type="bibr">41</xref>,<xref rid="c44" ref-type="bibr">44</xref>,<xref rid="c45" ref-type="bibr">45</xref>,<xref rid="c117" ref-type="bibr">117</xref></sup> In addition, expression levels of all targeted SLC transporters (i.e., Oat2, Urat1, Oatp1A1, Asbt, Ost <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i28" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, Npt1, and Npt4) and Bcrp (ABC transporter) were lower in rats with CKD, whereas expression levels of Mrp2 (ABC transporter) were higher and diminished with time after 5/6 nephrectomy (<xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">Figure 4A,B</xref>).<sup><xref rid="c94" ref-type="bibr">94</xref>,<xref rid="c118" ref-type="bibr">118</xref>–<xref rid="c120" ref-type="bibr">120</xref></sup></p><fig position="float" id="f3" orientation="portrait"><label>Figure 3.</label><caption><p>Renal transporter expression levels (percentage of control) in (A) human clinical glomerulonephritis, (B) experimental diabetic nephropathy in rats, and (C,D) experimental acute kidney injury in rats summarized from previous studies. In (A,B), the light blue shading below the horizontal line at 100 indicates value <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i29" display="inline" alttext="less than 100 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&lt;100%</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and light purple shading above the horizontal line at 100 indicates value <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i30" display="inline" alttext="greater than 100 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&gt;100%</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>. All data used to construct the figures were extracted from the literature (see <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref> and Excel Table S4 for references). Numbers in the figure are ordered according to <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref> references. The numbers of people or animals included in the reviewed studies are provided in <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref>. Note: BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; MRP/Mrp, multidrug resistance proteins; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; OATP/Oatp, organic anion-transporting polypeptides; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; SLE, glomerulonephritis secondary to systemic lupus erythematosus; SLE-C, SLE-control; SVV, glomerulonephritis secondary to small vessel vasculitis; SVV-C, SVV-control.</p></caption><alt-text>Figure 3A is a clustered bar graph titled Clinical glomerulonephritis, plotting protein level percentage of control, ranging from 0 to 210 in increments of 30 (y-axis) across O A T P 1 A 2, M R P 2, B C R P, and P-glycoprotein (x-axis) for S V V, S V V-C, S L E, and S L E-C. Figure 3B is a clustered bar graph titled Experimental diabetic nephropathy, plotting protein level percentage of control, ranging from 0 to 300 in increments of 50 (y-axis) across P-glycoprotein (x-axis) for uninephrectomy and uninephrectomy plus diabetes. Figure 3C is a graph titled Experimental acute kidney injury, plotting protein or messenger ribonucleic acid percentage of control, ranging from 20 to 100 in increments of 20 (y-axis) across time (hours), ranging from 0 to 96 in increments of 12, 96 to 192 in increments of 96, 192 to 240 in increments of 24 (x-axis) for Oat 1, Oat 2, and Oatp1a1. Figure 3D is a graph titled Experimental acute kidney injury, plotting protein or messenger ribonucleic acid percentage of control, ranging from 100 to 600 in increments of 100 (y-axis) across time (hours), ranging from 72 to 240 in increments of 24 (x-axis) for Mrp1, Mrp2, and P-glycoprotein.</alt-text><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="float" orientation="portrait" </inline-formula>
</td></tr><tr><th colspan="5" align="left" id="t4c19" scope="row" headers="t4c1" rowspan="1"><italic toggle="yes">Ex vivo</italic> Experimental CKD</th></tr><tr><td align="left" id="t4c20" scope="row" headers="t4c1 t4c19" colspan="1" rowspan="1"> 13</td><td align="center" headers="t4c2 t4c20" colspan="1" rowspan="1">
<sup>
<xref rid="c44" ref-type="bibr">44</xref>
