</td></tr></tbody></table><table-wrap-foot><fn id="TF9"><p>Note: AKI, acute kidney injury; ASBT/Asbt, apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter; BCRP/bcrp, breast cancer resistance protein; CKD, chronic kidney disease; MRP/Mrp, multidrug resistance proteins; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; OATP/Oatp, organic anion-transporting polypeptides; OST, organic solute transporter; PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; SLE, systemic lupus erythematosus; SVV, small vessel vasculitis; URAT/Urat, urate transporter.</p></fn></table-wrap-foot></table-wrap><fig position="float" id="f4" orientation="portrait"><label>Figure 4.</label><caption><p>Expression levels of (A) current known renal transporters for PFAS transport and (B) candidate renal transporters for PFAS transport as a percentage of control in experimental chronic kidney failure in rats, summarized from reviewed studies. Light blue shading below the horizontal line at 100 indicates value <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i55" display="inline" alttext="less than 100 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&lt;100%</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and light purple shading above the horizontal line at 100 indicates value <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i56" display="inline" alttext="greater than 100 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">&gt;100%</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>. All data used to construct the figures were extracted from <italic toggle="yes">ex vivo</italic> studies from the literature (see <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref> and Excel Table S4 for references). Numbers in the figure are ordered according to <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref> references. Numbers of animals in the reviewed studies are provided in <xref rid="t4" ref-type="table">Table 4</xref>. Note: ASBT/Asbt, apical sodium-dependent bile acid transporter; BCRP/Bcrp, breast cancer resistance protein; MRP/Mrp, multidrug resistance proteins; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; OST, organic solute transporter; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; PFAS, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances; URAT/Urat, urate transporter.</p></caption><alt-text>Figure 4A is a line graph titled Known transporters for P F A S transport, plotting protein or messenger ribonucleic acid percentage of control, ranging from 0 to 100 in increments of 20 (y-axis) across time (weeks), ranging from 0 to 25 in increments of 5 (x-axis) for Oat 1, Oat 2, Oat 3, Urat 1, Oatp1a1, Asbt, and Ost lowercase alpha or beta. Figure 4B is a graph titles Candidate transporters for P F A S transport, ranging from 40 to 240 in increments of 40 (y-axis) across time (weeks), ranging from 3 to 9 in unit increments (x-axis) for Bcrp, Mrp2, Mrp1, and P-glycoprotein.</alt-text><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="ehp11885_f4.jpg"><?image-name ehp11885_f4.jpg?><?image-size 72381?><?image-md5 b09d1f4ae23862768cef5993c5e36921?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 1017?><?image-original-width 2400?><?image-scaled-height 356?><?image-scaled-width 840?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/b09d1f4ae238/ehp11885_f4.jpg?><?thumb-name ehp11885_f4.gif?><?thumb-size 5628?><?thumb-md5 7f8976812a7748642fb3ec9ae8186aed?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 80?><?thumb-scaled-width 189?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/7f8976812a77/ehp11885_f4.gif?></graphic></fig></sec><sec id="s3.4"><title>PBPK Modeling in Populations with Kidney Disease</title><p>We identified 258 papers from the search for PFAS-related PBPK models. After exclusion of 221 papers that did not develop PBPK models for PFAS, 37 studies that reported on PFAS-specific PBPK models were included in this review (Excel Table S3). Among these 37 studies, 9 did not consider renal reabsorption in their PBPK models,<sup><xref rid="c121" ref-type="bibr">121</xref>–<xref rid="c129" ref-type="bibr">129</xref></sup> and 24 of them considered renal reabsorption using a transporter maximum (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i57" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript lowercase m end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>T</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, representing transporter saturation) and an affinity constant (<inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i58" display="inline" alttext="lowercase italic k begin subscript lowercase t end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>k</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">t</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>, representing transporter uptake), but none of them were transporter-specific.<sup><xref rid="c130" ref-type="bibr">130</xref>–<xref rid="c153" ref-type="bibr">153</xref></sup> Four publications included transporter-specific pathways, with 2 for PFAS in fish,<sup><xref rid="c154" ref-type="bibr">154</xref>,<xref rid="c155" ref-type="bibr">155</xref></sup> and 2 for PFAS in male rats.<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref>,<xref rid="c156" ref-type="bibr">156</xref></sup></p><p>PBPK models are useful tools to simulate the processes of absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) of chemicals in different organs and tissues in animals and humans.<sup><xref rid="c157" ref-type="bibr">157</xref></sup> In addition, PBPK models provide a link between external exposure and internal toxicokinetics and can be used in two different frameworks. First, these models can help characterize the relationship between exposure dose and the effects on the chemical concentrations in the target organs.<sup><xref rid="c158" ref-type="bibr">158</xref></sup> Second, PBPK models can predict the external exposure using human biomonitoring data, such as chemical levels in blood or urine.<sup><xref rid="c159" ref-type="bibr">159</xref></sup> This inverse prediction can also be used in estimating the reference dose in support of risk assessment.<sup><xref rid="c160" ref-type="bibr">160</xref></sup> Previous studies have revealed that PFOA strongly binds to serum albumin, resulting in a long half-life in human blood<sup><xref rid="c161" ref-type="bibr">161</xref></sup> and that active renal reabsorption contributes to sex- and species-specific differences in PFAS half-lives.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup></p><p>The permeability-limited PFOA-PBPK model for male rat developed by Cheng and Ng included four kidney transporters (i.e., Oat 1, Oat 3, Oatp1a1, and Ost <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i59" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>).<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> Although three of the four transporters (but not Ost <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i60" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) showed significant changes in expression levels during experimentally simulated kidney diseases in previous studies,<sup><xref rid="c103" ref-type="bibr">103</xref></sup> only Oat1 and Oat3 had significant changes at the same time points (<xref rid="f3" ref-type="fig">Figures 3</xref> and <xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">4</xref>). In addition, the Cheng and Ng model<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> included glomerular filtration as a pathway for PFAS excretion using a parameter for GFR, which might change significantly during the development of kidney disease. In this section, we describe using the Cheng and Ng model to simulate changes in transporter expression levels (by setting the clearance rate constant of Oat1 and Oat3 to 20% of the model default), in GFR (by setting GFR to 33%<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> and 5%<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> of the model default), and in albumin concentrations (by setting albumin concentration to 70% of the model default) to explore how such changes during kidney disease could affect PFOA toxicokinetics in the male rat. For transporter levels, the changes of Oat1 and Oat3 expression at 6 wk in <xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">Figure 4A</xref> were used to represent the worst-case scenario.</p><p>Serum PFOA toxicokinetics modeled using the modified Cheng and Ng PBPK model (<xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5</xref>) showed biphasic patterns with fast initial clearance, during which the serum half-life (also called the apparent half-life) was short, and slow terminal clearance during which the half-life (also called the terminal half-life, <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i61" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript 1 by 2 infinity end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>∞</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>) was long. The <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i62" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript 1 by 2 infinity end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>∞</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> reflected the rate and extent of elimination after the chemical or drug was accumulated, distributed, and reached pseudo-equilibrium. The serum PFOA <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i63" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript 1 by 2 infinity end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>∞</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was longer in rats with decreased levels of Oat 1 and Oat 3 and GFR compared with healthy rats (<xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5A,B</xref>) with normal levels. In addition, both the decrease of transporter expression levels (i.e., Oat1 and Oat3) and GFR resulted in higher PFOA concentrations in plasma than those in healthy rats. PFOA toxicokinetics were more influenced by changes in GFR than in transporter expression levels, whereas serum PFOA <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i64" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript 1 by 2 infinity end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>∞</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> was more influenced by changes in transporter expression levels than GFR (<xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5A,B</xref>). However, it is noteworthy that the modeled PFOA toxicokinetics ignored the influence of reabsorption transporter (Oatp1a1) changes in disease owing to a lack of data for this polypeptide (at the same time points as Oat1 and Oat3). Serum PFOA <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i65" display="inline" alttext="uppercase italic t begin subscript 1 by 2 infinity end subscript"><mml:mrow><mml:msub><mml:mrow><mml:mi>t</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>1</mml:mn><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mn>2</mml:mn><mml:mo>∞</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:mrow></mml:msub></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> were comparable between rats with 70% decreased albumin concentrations and healthy rats; however, the half-life at the initial phase was shorter in rats with decreased serum albumin concentration (<xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5C</xref>), indicating a faster PFOA elimination due to the decrease of albumin. Lower serum PFAS measured in humans with albuminuria provides inferential evidence that this elimination difference also pertains to humans.<sup><xref rid="c162" ref-type="bibr">162</xref></sup> It should be noted that the model results were based on a single simulated exposure; in the real world, humans have continuous exposures, primarily by ingestion and inhalation, as well as complex patterns of excretion in progressive stages of kidney disease. This means, in the real world, the human body burden of PFAS may be elevated compared with the simulated scenario, particularly in populations with specific kidney diseases, although human populations with any albuminuria could have lower PFAS concentrations in serum than those with normal or mildly impaired renal function.</p><fig position="float" id="f5" orientation="portrait"><label>Figure 5.</label><caption><p>Simulations of PFOA toxicokinetics using the Cheng and Ng model<sup><xref rid="c13" ref-type="bibr">13</xref></sup> for male rats. (A) Effect of decreased expression levels of Oat1 and Oat3 (the clearance rate constants for Oat1 and Oat3 were set to 20% of the original model default value, and the transporter expression changes were obtained from <xref rid="f4" ref-type="fig">Figure 4A</xref>) on PFOA half-life. (B) Effect of decreased GFR (by setting GFR to 33%<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> and 5%<sup><xref rid="c43" ref-type="bibr">43</xref></sup> of the model default) on serum PFOA half-life. (C) Effect of decreased albumin concentration mimicking albuminuria (by setting albumin concentration to 70% of the model default) on PFOA half-life. All data used to construct the figures are provided in Excel Table S5. Note: GFR, glomerular filtration rate; OAT/Oat, organic anion transporters; PFOA, perfluorooctanoic acid.</p></caption><alt-text>Figures 5A, 5B, and 5C are line graphs titled Transporter expression, Glomerular filtration rates, and Albuminuria, plotting P F O A concentration in plasma (nanograms per gram), ranging as 10 begin superscript 3 end superscript and 10 begin superscript 4 end superscript (y-axis) across Time (min), ranging from 0 to 240 in increments of 60 (x-axis) for Figure 5A: healthy male rat, 20% clearance; for Figure 5B: healthy male rat, 5% and 33% glomerular filtration rates; and for Figure 5C: healthy male rat, 70% albumin, respectively.</alt-text><graphic xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" position="float" orientation="portrait" xlink:href="ehp11885_f5.jpg"><?image-name ehp11885_f5.jpg?><?image-size 87351?><?image-md5 f041abda71c8b72d623c27affad84239?><?image-image-server-status LOAD_COMPLETED?><?image-original-height 2021?><?image-original-width 2400?><?image-scaled-height 707?><?image-scaled-width 840?><?image-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/f041abda71c8/ehp11885_f5.jpg?><?thumb-name ehp11885_f5.gif?><?thumb-size 3028?><?thumb-md5 d470da0a1684eb6bf46099523a3c9045?><?thumb-image-server-status NEVER_LOAD?><?thumb-scaled-height 84?><?thumb-scaled-width 100?><?thumb-cloudpmc-urn urn:cdn:blobs/0b50/10328216/d470da0a1684/ehp11885_f5.gif?></graphic></fig></sec></sec><sec id="s4"><title>Discussion</title><sec id="s4.1"><title>Research Gaps and Promising Directions for PFAS Interactions with Renal Transporters</title><p>As knowledge around PFAS renal elimination has progressed to the molecular and cellular levels, a number of human and rat renal transporters located at the basolateral and apical membranes of proximal tubular cells have been investigated for their interactions and affinities with PFAS molecules.<sup><xref rid="c22" ref-type="bibr">22</xref>,<xref rid="c26" ref-type="bibr">26</xref>,<xref rid="c27" ref-type="bibr">27</xref></sup> Several human and rat renal transporters, such as OATs/Oats,<sup><xref rid="c38" ref-type="bibr">38</xref></sup> OST <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i66" display="inline" alttext="lowercase alpha or beta"><mml:mrow><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">α</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">β</mml:mi></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>,<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> and ASBT<sup><xref rid="c21" ref-type="bibr">21</xref></sup> have been shown to facilitate PFAS transport with medium-to-high affinities, and these affinities were also related to PFAS structures. To date, more renal uptake transporters (e.g., most SLC transporters) than efflux transporters (e.g., ABC transporters) have been studied in human and rat systems (<xref rid="f2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>), based on interest in the renal reabsorption pathway extending PFAS half-lives. As a result, knowledge on the interactions between renal efflux transporters and PFAS remains incomplete. Some branched isomers of PFAS (e.g., of PFOS and PFOA) have been shown to have longer or much shorter half-lives than corresponding linear isomers in humans<sup><xref rid="c163" ref-type="bibr">163</xref></sup>; however, to the best of our knowledge, no related studies have been done in terms of renal transporters. In addition, the huge PFAS family includes not only the anionic PFAAs that are the ones studied until now, but also polyfluorinated substances and PFAS that are cationic or zwitterionic at environmental pH values.<sup><xref rid="c164" ref-type="bibr">164</xref></sup> Renal organic cation transporters (OCTs, from the <italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>22 gene family) and multidrug and toxicant extrusion proteins (MATEs, from the <italic toggle="yes">SLC</italic>47 gene family) could be involved in cationic and zwitterionic PFAS transport.</p><p>Selection of an appropriate experimental model is an important consideration for studies of PFAS toxicokinetics and toxicodynamics and to understand the relevance for human and wildlife risk assessments. For example, in zebrafish, PFOA renal elimination is probably mediated by transporters other than Oatp1d1.<sup><xref rid="c67" ref-type="bibr">67</xref></sup> Although the endocrine system has been reported to be very similar in teleost fish and mammals, the role of the Oatp1 transporter in transporting PFAS differed substantially between zebrafish and mammals, and the substrate-binding sites of Oatp1d1 and mammalian OATP1 differed to some extent.<sup><xref rid="c67" ref-type="bibr">67</xref></sup> Therefore, zebrafish cannot be directly used as a model to study mammalian OAT/Oat transport systems with respect to PFAS transport and disposition.<sup><xref rid="c67" ref-type="bibr">67</xref></sup> Only a handful of PFAS, most notably PFOA, have been studied in terms of their interactions with renal transporters. Studies of more PFAS, especially the current-use PFAS (including shorter-chain PFAS and replacements, such as GenX) are also needed for better coverage of the role of transporters across the structurally diverse PFAS family.</p><p>This review has focused on direct PFAS transport by kidney transporters. Although we did not include the topic of transporter inhibition by PFAS, PFOA has been shown to induce Bcrp expression in mice and may inhibit the human BCRP transporter function at concentrations that exceed levels observed in humans.<sup><xref rid="c165" ref-type="bibr">165</xref></sup> In addition, nanomolar levels of GenX inhibited P-gp and Bcrp but not Mrp2 transporter activities in male and female rats, and GenX reduced P-gp and BCRP transporter activity in human cells.<sup><xref rid="c166" ref-type="bibr">166</xref></sup> Inhibition of transporter activity by a specific PFAS does not necessarily mean that the transporter can transport that specific PFAS. However, the inhibition of a transporter could influence its ability to transport other substrates (e.g., endogenous ligands or other PFAS). Adding to the complexity, different PFAS may compete with each other for binding to transporters.<sup><xref rid="c167" ref-type="bibr">167</xref></sup> Therefore, future work should also consider the inhibitory effect of PFAS on transporters and competitive binding, which are important considerations in the context of PFAS mixtures.</p></sec><sec id="s4.2"><title>More Work Needed regarding Renal Transporters in Health and Disease</title><p>We have proposed a candidate list of renal transporters that have the potential for PFAS transport as a result of this review (<xref rid="t3" ref-type="table">Table 3</xref>). This list will lengthen as the knowledge of PFAS-biomarker relationships, as well as information on the endogenous substrates of proteins, progresses. Animal experimental work addresses one aspect of the role of expression-level changes in renal transporters altered by kidney disease state. Overall, expression levels of SLC transporters (e.g., Oat1, Oat3, Oatp1a1), which are mainly uptake transporters, initially decreased and sometimes fully or partially returned to normal as the recovery progressed in surgically induced AKI and CKD, whereas expression levels of ABC transporters (e.g., Mrp 1, Bcrp, P-gp), which are mainly efflux transporters, had specific outcomes. For example, expression levels of BCRP and P-gp in humans with clinical glomerulonephritis was <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i67" display="inline" alttext="approximately 70 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>70</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> and <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i68" display="inline" alttext="approximately 40 percent"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>40</mml:mn><mml:mo>%</mml:mo></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> lower, respectively,<sup><xref rid="c108" ref-type="bibr">108</xref></sup> whereas levels of P-gp in experimental rats with diabetic nephropathy was 50% higher.<sup><xref rid="c42" ref-type="bibr">42</xref></sup> Currently, clinical evidence for transporter changes in kidney diseases is scarce. PFAS-transporter roles and changes as a function of disease or developmental stages, as well as interaction with and modifications by other toxicants and medications, are poorly understood. In addition, specific changes in GFR, along with transporter expression changing during kidney disease, are also important for predicting PFAS toxicokinetics. Menstrual blood loss, breast-feeding, and childbirth are sex-specific excretion routes for PFAS<sup><xref rid="c65" ref-type="bibr">65</xref>,<xref rid="c168" ref-type="bibr">168</xref></sup> that could contribute to sex differences, but sex differences in the changes in expression levels of renal transporters may also play a role. These major research gaps are critical to understanding the interindividual variabilities in PFAS toxicokinetics and identifying particularly sensitive populations.</p></sec><sec id="s4.3"><title>More Work Needed to Advance PBPK Models</title><p>PFOA toxicokinetics have been studied in patients with advanced cancer by Convertino et al., and they posited that the previously published half-lives of PFOA in healthy humans (i.e., 2–3 y<sup><xref rid="c52" ref-type="bibr">52</xref></sup>) were significantly longer than actual half-lives.<sup><xref rid="c52" ref-type="bibr">52</xref></sup> However, a limitation of that study was that they did not consider the influence of disease on PFOA elimination by the human body. This could be important because the renal transporter protein levels and GFR could be significantly altered in humans with kidney disease,<sup><xref rid="c169" ref-type="bibr">169</xref></sup> which will further strongly influence PFAS elimination from the human body. In addition, the fundamental limitation reported in the conclusions by Convertino et al.<sup><xref rid="c124" ref-type="bibr">124</xref></sup> concerning the PFOA half-life was the use of acute chemotherapeutic PFOA doses, which are orders of magnitude greater than chronic environmental exposures. To the degree that Convertino et al.<sup><xref rid="c124" ref-type="bibr">124</xref></sup> half-life data in terminally ill cancer patients can be interpreted, the apparently shorter half-lives most likely reflect a limit of reabsorption kinetics and binding capacity that operates at some point in the large exposure difference between the highest chronic environmental and the acute chemotherapeutic doses. More accurate, quantitative, and comprehensive characterization of renal tubular secretion and reabsorption pathways in both health and disease is required to further advance PFAS-PBPK models, especially for understanding the impacts of kidney disease on PFAS toxicokinetics. Appropriate exposure doses are also critical to toxicokinetic studies.</p><p>Fenton et al.<sup><xref rid="c170" ref-type="bibr">170</xref></sup> showed that GFR had sex- and age-dependent patterns, with significantly higher GFR in human males than females (92.0 vs. <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i69" display="inline" alttext="88.1 milliliters per minute per 173 meters squared"><mml:mrow><mml:mn>88.1</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>min per </mml:mtext><mml:mn>1.73</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>), in a healthy population from 20 to 80 years of age. In addition, GFR was <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i70" display="inline" alttext="approximately 100 milliliters per minute per 1.73 meters squared"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>∼</mml:mo><mml:mn>100</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>min per </mml:mtext><mml:mn>1.73</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> until 35 years of age, followed by linear decreases along age increases, with a faster decline in females compared with males, and the major proportion of the healthy population <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i71" display="inline" alttext="greater than 60 years"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&gt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>60</mml:mn><mml:mtext> years</mml:mtext></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> of age had a GFR <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i72" display="inline" alttext="less than 60 milliliters per minute per 1.73 meters squared"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>60</mml:mn><mml:mspace width="0.3em"/><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">mL</mml:mi><mml:mo>/</mml:mo><mml:mtext>min per </mml:mtext><mml:mn>1.73</mml:mn><mml:msup><mml:mrow><mml:mtext> </mml:mtext><mml:mi mathvariant="normal">m</mml:mi></mml:mrow><mml:mrow><mml:mn>2</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:msup></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula>.<sup><xref rid="c170" ref-type="bibr">170</xref></sup> Variability in GFR in healthy populations could result in variability in PFAS toxicokinetics. According to <xref rid="f5" ref-type="fig">Figure 5B</xref>, using a lower GFR in the model resulted in slower PFAS elimination (i.e., longer half-lives) and higher plasma PFAS concentrations in male rats compared with healthy male rats. Similarly, in humans, senior groups could have slower PFAS elimination (i.e., longer half-lives) and higher PFAS concentrations accumulated in blood compared with people <inline-formula><mml:math xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="i73" display="inline" alttext="less than 35"><mml:mrow><mml:mo>&lt;</mml:mo><mml:mn>35</mml:mn></mml:mrow></mml:math></inline-formula> years of age. In addition, males have slightly faster PFAS elimination than females via the glomerular filtration pathway; however, transporter-facilitated pathways also contribute to sex differences in PFAS renal elimination.<sup><xref rid="c20" ref-type="bibr">20</xref></sup> There is limited information on the variability in transporter expression levels in humans. Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, there are no transporter-specific PFAS-PBPK models for humans so far. In this review, we used an existing PFOA-PBPK model for male rats to investigate the influence of changes in transporter levels, GFR, and serum albumin on PFOA toxicokinetics. However, translating these conclusions across species can be difficult owing to species differences. Therefore, more work is needed to understand the impacts of variability in transporter expression changes on the variability in PFAS toxicokinetics in humans.</p></sec></sec><sec id="s5"><title>Conclusion</title><p>Research on PFAS transport by renal transporters is increasing, yet the diversity of investigated PFAS and transporter types remains limited. Evidence supports that renal reabsorption is responsible for some sex- and species-specific differences in PFAS half-lives. Therefore, renal transporters that facilitate reabsorption have received more attention compared with those that facilitate excretion. Further studies are needed to explore additional renal transporters to fully understand the renal elimination mechanisms and to include more PFAS compounds, particularly the current-use PFAS, such as shorter-chain PFAS, to advance knowledge of their renal transport processes. In this review, we have identified seven potential PFAS transporters that should be prioritized for experimental evaluation. Kidney disease status can alter renal transporter expression levels and, therefore, influence the renal PFAS elimination; however, very limited work has been conducted investigating how various kidney diseases impact PFAS toxicokinetics. Moreover, research gaps exist in understanding the expression changes of renal transporters as a function of developmental stages at different life stages. PBPK models are useful tools for simulating PFAS toxicokinetics; however, they require PFAS-specific data and complex parameters describing ADME mechanisms. Unfortunately, most of the data and parameters specific to PFAS are not currently available. Alternatively, <italic toggle="yes">in silico</italic> and <italic toggle="yes">in vitro</italic> methods could aid in PBPK parameterization. Progress on more mechanisms of toxicokinetics for the broad PFAS class in kidney health and disease status is critically needed to comprehensively assess human health risk and identify vulnerable populations.</p></sec><sec sec-type="supplementary-material"><title>Supplementary Material</title><supplementary-material position="float" content-type="local-data" orientation="portrait"><media <volume>130</volume>(<issue>4</issue>):<elocation-id>41303</elocation-id>, PMID: , <pub-id pub-id-type="doi">10.1289/EHP11149</pub-id>.<pub-id pub-id-type="pmid">35475651</pub-id><pub-id pub-id-type="pmcid">PMC9044975</pub-id></mixed-citation></ref><ref id="c9"><label>9.</label><mixed-citation publication-type="journal"><person-group person-group-type="author"><string-name name-style="western"><surname>Zhao</surname>
